I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the lexicographically smallest subsequence of length K from string S.

Key insights:
- We want the smallest lexicographical result
- We need exactly K characters from the original string
- The subsequence maintains the relative order of characters from the original string

## Approach Analysis

This is a classic greedy problem. The key insight is that we want to build the result character by character from left to right, making optimal choices at each step.

At each position, we want to choose the smallest possible character that still allows us to complete the subsequence of length K.

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Greedy Strategy**: At each position, try to pick the smallest character that won't make it impossible to form a valid subsequence of length K.

2. **Key Observation**: If we're at position i and want to pick a character at this step, we need to ensure that after picking this character, we can still form a subsequence of length (K - current_length) from the remaining part of the string.

3. **Remaining Characters Check**: For a character at position j, we need at least (K - current_length) characters remaining after position j (including position j).

4. **Algorithm**:
   - Use a stack-based approach
   - For each character in the string, decide whether to include it or not
   - We can remove characters from the stack if we find a smaller character that still allows us to complete the subsequence

5. **Implementation Logic**:
   - We maintain a result stack
   - We traverse the string from left to right
   - For each character, we check if we can remove characters from the stack to get a smaller lexicographical result
   - We can remove a character from the stack if the remaining characters after that position can still form a valid subsequence of the required length

## Detailed Algorithm

For each position i (0 to n-1):
- Let `remaining = n - i` (characters left from position i onwards)
- Let `needed = K - len(result)` (characters needed in result after this position)
- If `remaining >= needed`, then we can include the current character
- We can remove characters from the result stack if:
  - The character at the top of stack is greater than current character
  - And there are enough characters remaining to complete the subsequence

```python
def solve(s, k):
    n = len(s)
    # Use a stack to build the result
    stack = []
    
    for i in range(n):
        # Characters remaining after position i
        remaining = n - i
        # Characters needed in result after current position
        needed = k - len(stack)
        
        # If we still need characters and there are enough remaining characters
        while stack and stack[-1] > s[i] and remaining - 1 >= needed:
            # We can remove the top of stack since we can get a smaller lexicographical result
            stack.pop()
        
        # Add current character if we haven't reached length k
        if len(stack) < k:
            stack.append(s[i])
    
    return ''.join(stack)
```

```python
def solve(s, k):
    n = len(s)
    stack = []
    
    for i in range(n):
        # The number of characters left after position i
        left = n - i
        # Characters needed in the result (after current position)
        needed = k - len(stack)
        
        # We can remove from stack if:
        # 1. Top of stack is greater than current character
        # 2. There are enough characters left to still form a valid subsequence
        while stack and stack[-1] > s[i] and left >= needed:
            stack.pop()
        
        # If we haven't reached the required length, add current character
        if len(stack) < k:
            stack.append(s[i])
    
    return ''.join(stack)
```

```python
def smallest_subsequence(s, k):
    stack = []
    n = len(s)
    
    for i in range(n):
        # characters remaining after current position (i+1 to end)
        remaining = n - i - 1
        # characters needed after including s[i]
        needed = k - len(stack) - 1
        
        # if we can still form a valid subsequence, remove larger characters
        while stack and stack[-1] > s[i] and remaining >= needed:
            stack.pop()
        
        # if we haven't reached k characters, add current character
        if len(stack) < k:
            stack.append(s[i])
    
    return ''.join(stack)
```

Akhil comes across a string S of length N. He started wondering about the smallest lexicographical subsequence of string S of length K.
A subsequence of a string is formed by deleting some characters (possibly none) from it's original string.
A string A is said to be lexicographically smaller than the string B of the same length if at the first position where A and B differ, A contains a letter which appears earlier in the dictionary than the corresponding letter in B.

First line of each test case will contain string S

------ Constraints ------ 

$1 ≤ T ≤ 5$
$1 ≤ K ≤ N$
$S consists of lowercase English alphabet characters, i.e. from 'a' to 'z'.$

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the lexicographically smallest subsequence of length K from string S.

Key points:
- A subsequence means we can remove characters (but keep the relative order)
- We want the smallest lexicographical result
- The result must have exactly K characters

## Approach

This is a classic problem that can be solved using a greedy approach with a stack (or deque). The key insight is:

1. We want to build the result character by character
2. At each position, we should choose the smallest possible character that still allows us to form a valid subsequence of length K
3. We need to ensure we have enough characters remaining to complete the subsequence

## Algorithm Steps

1. Use a stack to build the result
2. For each character in the string:
   - While stack is not empty, the top character is greater than current character, and we still have enough characters left to form a subsequence of length K, pop from stack
   - Add current character to stack
3. After processing all characters, we might have more than K characters, so we need to truncate to K

## Detailed Logic

For each position i in the string:
- If we have more than K characters in our result stack, we can't have more than K
- Before adding current character, we can remove characters from the stack if:
  - The top character is greater than current character
  - We still have enough remaining characters to complete the subsequence (i.e., we can reach K characters)

The condition for removal: after removing a character at index j from stack, we need to ensure we can still form K characters total. If we have `stack_size` characters already, and we want K total, we need at least `(K - stack_size)` characters remaining after current position.

Wait, let me refine this:

After processing up to position i, we have `stack_size` characters. We need to ensure that after current position, we can still get enough characters to reach K. So we need: `stack_size + (n - i - 1) >= K` after adding current character.

## Step-by-step Solution

```python
def solve(s, k):
    n = len(s)
    stack = []
    
    for i, char in enumerate(s):
        # While stack is not empty and top element is greater than current
        # and we can still form a valid subsequence of length K
        while stack and stack[-1] > char and len(stack) + (n - i - 1) >= k:
            stack.pop()
        
        # If we haven't reached k characters yet, add current character
        if len(stack) < k:
            stack.append(char)
    
    return ''.join(stack)
```

Let me trace through examples:

**Example 1: "abdc", K=3**
- i=0, char='a': stack=[] → add 'a' → stack=['a']
- i=1, char='b': stack=['a'] → 'a'<'b' → add 'b' → stack=['a','b'] 
- i=2, char='d': stack=['a','b'] → 'b'<'d' → add 'd' → stack=['a','b','d']
- i=3, char='c': stack=['a','b','d'] → 'd'>'c' and len(stack)=3, (4-3-1)=0, 3+0=3 >= 3 → pop 'd' → stack=['a','b']
  → now len(stack)=2 < 3, so add 'c' → stack=['a','b','c']

Result: "abc" ✓

**Example 2: "bacb", K=2**
- i=0, char='b': stack=[] → add 'b' → stack=['b']
- i=1, char='a': stack=['b'] → 'b'>'a' and len(stack)=1, (4-1-1)=2, 1+2=3 >= 2 → pop 'b' → stack=[]
  → len(stack)=0 < 2 → add 'a' → stack=['a']
- i=2, char='c': stack=['a'] → 'a'<'c' → add 'c' → stack=['a','c']
- i=3, char='b': stack=['a','c'] → 'c'>'b' and len(stack)=2, (4-3-1)=0, 2+0=2 >= 2 → pop 'c' → stack=['a']
  → len(stack)=1 < 2 → add 'b' → stack=['a','b']

Result: "ab" ✓

```python
def solve(s, k):
    n = len(s)
    stack = []
    
    for i, char in enumerate(s):
        # While we can remove the top element (it's greater than current)
        # and we still have enough characters left to reach K
        while stack and stack[-1] > char and len(stack) + (n - i - 1) >= k:
            stack.pop()
        
        # If we haven't reached the required length K, add current character
        if len(stack) < k:
            stack.append(char)
    
    return ''.join(stack)

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    s = input().strip()
    k = int(input())
    print(solve(s, k))
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the lexicographically smallest subsequence of length K from string S.

Key points:
- We must select exactly K characters from S (maintaining their relative order)
- The result should be lexicographically smallest
- We can only remove characters, not rearrange them

## Approach

This is a classic problem that can be solved using a greedy approach with a stack/monotonic stack concept.

**Intuition**: 
- To get the lexicographically smallest subsequence, we want smaller characters earlier in the string
- If we have a choice between two characters, we should pick the smaller one
- However, we need to ensure we can still form a subsequence of length K

**Algorithm**:
1. Use a stack to build the result
2. For each character in the string:
   - While the stack is not empty, the top character is greater than current character, and we still have enough characters left to form a subsequence of length K, pop from stack
   - Add current character to stack
3. The stack will contain the lexicographically smallest subsequence

**Key insight**: 
- We can only remove characters, not rearrange
- We need to ensure that after removing some characters, we still have enough characters left to reach length K
- When deciding to pop from stack, we need to check if we can still form length K

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Initialize an empty stack
2. For each character in S:
   - While stack is not empty, and stack[-1] > current_char, and we have enough characters remaining to form K-length subsequence, pop from stack
   - Add current character to stack
3. If stack size is less than K, we need to extend it (but this shouldn't happen with the right logic)
4. Return the stack content (first K characters)

**Condition for popping**: 
- stack[-1] > current_char (current is smaller)
- we have enough characters remaining to reach K: (len(stack) - 1) + (len(remaining)) >= K

```python
def solve(s, k):
    stack = []
    n = len(s)
    
    for i, char in enumerate(s):
        # While stack is not empty, top element is greater than current,
        # and we can still form a valid subsequence of length K
        while stack and stack[-1] > char and (len(stack) + (n - i - 1)) >= k:
            stack.pop()
        
        # Add current character only if we don't have enough characters to reach K
        if len(stack) < k:
            stack.append(char)
    
    # If we still need more characters, add remaining ones
    while len(stack) < k:
        stack.append(s[len(stack)])
    
    return ''.join(stack)
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the lexicographically smallest subsequence of length K from string S.

A subsequence is formed by deleting some characters (possibly none) from the original string, maintaining the relative order of remaining characters.

## Key Insight

To get the lexicographically smallest subsequence:
- We want to pick the smallest possible characters at each position
- However, we need to ensure we have enough characters left to complete the subsequence of length K

## Strategy

This is a classic greedy problem. The approach is:

1. We need to build the result character by character
2. For each position, we want to pick the smallest possible character that allows us to complete the remaining K positions
3. At each step, we need to ensure that after picking a character at position i, we still have enough remaining characters to form a subsequence of length (K - current_length)

## Algorithm

For each position in the result:
1. Look at characters from current position to the end of string
2. For each character, check if we can form a valid subsequence of length (K - current_length) after picking this character
3. Since we want lexicographically smallest, we pick the smallest character that satisfies the constraint

More specifically:
- We can pick a character at index i only if (remaining_length) ≤ (total remaining characters from i onwards)
- After picking a character at position i, we need to ensure we can form (K - current_position) more characters from the remaining string

Actually, let me think of a simpler approach:

**Greedy with constraints:**
- We need to select K characters maintaining order
- For position j (0-indexed), we need to pick a character at some index i ≥ j
- After picking character at index i, we need to be able to pick (K - j - 1) more characters from indices > i
- So we need: (length of S) - i - 1 ≥ (K - j - 1)
- This means: length of S - i - 1 ≥ K - j - 1
- So: i ≤ length of S - K + j

Therefore, at position j, we can pick any character from position j to position (n - K + j).

## Step-by-step Solution

```python
def solve(s, k):
    n = len(s)
    result = []
    remaining_to_pick = k
    
    for i in range(k):
        # We need to pick a character at some index >= i
        # After picking, we need to have enough characters to complete the rest
        # If we pick at position pos, we need: (n - pos - 1) >= (k - i - 1)
        # So pos <= n - k + i
        
        min_pos = i
        max_pos = n - k + i
        
        # Find the smallest character in range [min_pos, max_pos]
        best_char = s[min_pos]
        best_idx = min_pos
        
        for j in range(min_pos + 1, max_pos + 1):
            if s[j] < best_char:
                best_char = s[j]
                best_idx = j
        
        result.append(best_char)
        
        # We picked at best_idx, so effectively remove it from consideration
        # But for the next iteration, we need to consider only positions after best_idx
        # Actually simpler: just maintain the current position and advance
        
        # Actually, we don't remove, we just know the next available position is best_idx + 1
        # But our constraint already considers the positions
    
    return ''.join(result)
```

Wait, I need to reconsider. The above greedy approach might not work correctly because we're not properly maintaining the constraints.

Let me use a stack-based approach which is more efficient:

**Stack-based approach:**
- Use a stack to build result
- For each character in S:
  - While stack is not empty and current character is smaller than top of stack and we still have enough characters left to form the required length
  - Remove the top of stack
- Add current character if we haven't reached length K

```python
def solve(s, k):
    n = len(s)
    stack = []
    to_remove = n - k  # number of characters we can remove
    
    for i, c in enumerate(s):
        # While we can remove from stack and current char is smaller
        while stack and c < stack[-1] and to_remove > 0:
            stack.pop()
            to_remove -= 1
        stack.append(c)
    
    # If we have more than k characters, remove from end
    return ''.join(stack[:k])
```

Wait, this still might not be right. Let me think of the constraint again.

Actually, the stack-based approach is correct for this problem. The key insight is:
- We can remove at most (n - k) characters to get a subsequence of length k
- We want the lexicographically smallest, so we remove larger characters when we see smaller ones
- We can only remove if we have enough characters left to reach length k

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: S = "abdc", K = 3
- n = 4, to_remove = 1
- i=0, c='a': stack=[], add 'a' → stack=['a']
- i=1, c='b': b > 'a', add → stack=['a','b']  
- i=2, c='d': d > 'b', add → stack=['a','b','d']
- i=3, c='c': c < 'd' and to_remove > 0, so remove 'd', to_remove=0 → stack=['a','b']
- Now add 'c' → stack=['a','b','c']
- Result = "abc" ✓

Example 2: S = "bacb", K = 2  
- n = 4, to_remove = 2
- i=0, c='b': stack=[], add 'b' → stack=['b']
- i=1, c='a': a < 'b' and to_remove > 0, remove 'b', to_remove=1 → stack=[]
- add 'a' → stack=['a']
- i=2, c='c': c > 'a', add → stack=['a','c']
- i=3, c='b': b < 'c' and to_remove=1 > 0, remove 'c', to_remove=0 → stack=['a']
- add 'b' → stack=['a','b']
- Result = "ab" ✓

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the lexicographically smallest subsequence of length K from string S.

Key insights:
- We want the smallest lexicographical result
- We need exactly K characters from the original string
- We can only delete characters, not rearrange them
- The subsequence maintains the original order

## Approach Analysis

This is a classic "smallest lexicographical subsequence" problem. The key insight is that we can use a greedy approach with a stack-like structure.

**Intuition:**
- To get the lexicographically smallest result, we want to use smaller characters when possible
- If we have a choice, we should pick the smallest available character at each position
- However, we must ensure we can still form a subsequence of length K

**Algorithm:**
1. We need to maintain that after selecting K characters, we still have enough characters left to complete the subsequence
2. For each position, we decide whether to include the current character or skip it
3. We can use a greedy approach: at each step, if we can replace a previously chosen character with a smaller one (and still have enough characters left to complete the subsequence), we do so

**Detailed Strategy:**
- We work from left to right
- We maintain a stack (list) of characters we've selected so far
- For each character in the string:
  - While our stack is not empty, the top character is greater than the current character, and we still have enough characters remaining to form a valid subsequence of length K, we pop from the stack
  - This ensures we always have the lexicographically smallest sequence
  - Add the current character to the stack
- If the stack exceeds K characters, we need to remove excess from the end (we can only have K characters)

**Key Constraint:**
- When considering removing a character from the stack, we need to ensure that after removal, we still have enough characters remaining in the string to complete the subsequence of length K.

## Step-by-step Algorithm

1. Initialize an empty list for the result
2. For each character in the string:
   - While the result is not empty, the last character is greater than current character, and (remaining characters + current length of result) >= K, remove the last character
   - Add current character to result
3. If result length > K, remove excess from the end

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1: "abdc", K=3**
- i=0: 'a' → result = ['a']
- i=1: 'b' → result = ['a','b'] 
- i=2: 'd' → result = ['a','b','d'] 
- i=3: 'c' → c < d, and remaining characters (1) + current length (3) = 4 ≥ 3, so pop 'd' → ['a','b']
  → c < b? No, so add 'c' → ['a','b','c']
- Final result: "abc" ✓

**Example 2: "bacb", K=2**
- i=0: 'b' → result = ['b']
- i=1: 'a' → a < b, and remaining (3) + 1 = 4 ≥ 2, so pop 'b' → []
  → add 'a' → ['a']
- i=2: 'c' → result = ['a','c'] → length 2 = K, done
- Final result: "ac"? But expected is "ab"

Wait, let me reconsider. The expected output is "ab". 

Let me retrace: 
- "bacb", K=2
- We need to pick 2 characters maintaining order
- Possible subsequences: "ba", "bc", "ac", "ab", "bb" (but no duplicate b)

"ab" is valid: take position 1 ('a') and position 3 ('b')
"ac" is valid: take position 1 ('a') and position 2 ('c')
"bc" is valid: take position 0 ('b') and position 2 ('c')
"ba" is valid: take position 0 ('b') and position 1 ('a')

Lexicographically: "ab" < "ac" < "ba" < "bc"

So "ab" is the answer.

Akhil comes across a string S of length N. He started wondering about the smallest lexicographical subsequence of string S of length K.
A subsequence of a string is formed by deleting some characters (possibly none) from it's original string.
A string A is said to be lexicographically smaller than the string B of the same length if at the first position where A and B differ, A contains a letter which appears earlier in the dictionary than the corresponding letter in B.

------ Input ------ 

The first line of the input contains an integer T denoting the number of test cases. The description of T test cases follows:
First line of each test case will contain string S
Second line of each test case will contain an integer K.

------ Output ------ 

For each test case, output a single line containing the lexicographically smallest subsequence of S of length K.

------ Constraints ------ 

$1 ≤ T ≤ 5$
$1 ≤ K ≤ N$
$S consists of lowercase English alphabet characters, i.e. from 'a' to 'z'.$

Subtask #1 (10 points) : 1 ≤ |S| ≤ 100

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the lexicographically smallest subsequence of length K from a given string S.

Key insights:
- We want the smallest lexicographical result
- A subsequence maintains the original order of characters
- We need exactly K characters

## Approach

This is a classic greedy problem. The key insight is that we should try to make the leftmost character as small as possible, then the next one, and so on.

For each position in our result, we need to choose the smallest possible character that allows us to complete the subsequence of length K.

The rule: 
- For position i (0-indexed), we need to pick a character from S
- After picking this character at position i, we need to be able to pick (K-1-i) more characters from the remaining part of the string

## Algorithm

1. Use a greedy approach with a stack or by iterating through the string
2. For each character, decide whether to include it or skip it based on:
   - If including this character would allow us to form a valid subsequence of length K
   - If we can get a smaller character in the future, we should skip current ones

Actually, let me think of a simpler approach:

We can use the following greedy strategy:
- We iterate through the string and build our result
- At each position, we try to use the smallest possible character that allows us to form a valid subsequence
- We can't remove an already placed character, so we need to ensure we have enough characters left

Better approach:
- We can use a greedy algorithm where we maintain the current result and for each position, we choose the smallest character that still allows us to complete the subsequence

Final approach (standard solution for this problem):
- We iterate through the string
- We maintain a stack (result list)
- For each character, while we have space to remove characters from the stack and the current character is smaller than the last character in stack, we remove the larger character
- We ensure we don't go below K characters

Wait, let me reconsider with constraints:
- We need exactly K characters
- We need to preserve order

Standard solution:
1. Use a stack to build result
2. For each character in S:
   - While stack is not empty, the top of stack > current character, and we have enough characters left to form K-length subsequence, pop from stack
   - Add current character to stack
3. If stack size > K, remove from the end (we want exactly K)

Actually, let me implement the standard solution:

## Step-by-step Solution

```python
def solve(s, k):
    stack = []
    n = len(s)
    
    for i, char in enumerate(s):
        # While stack is not empty and top character > current character
        # and we can still form a valid subsequence (have enough characters left)
        while stack and stack[-1] > char and (len(s) - i) > (k - len(stack)):
            stack.pop()
        
        # If we haven't reached K characters yet, add current character
        if len(stack) < k:
            stack.append(char)
    
    return ''.join(stack)
```

```python
def solve(s, k):
    stack = []
    n = len(s)
    
    for i, char in enumerate(s):
        # While stack is not empty and top character > current character
        # and we have enough characters remaining to form a valid subsequence
        while stack and stack[-1] > char and (len(s) - i) >= (k - len(stack)):
```
